I still show up sometimes, because I’ve known those women for a while and I don’t want to seem rude or disappear completely, but I’m not as engaged. I’ll sit there and listen, maybe say a little, but I’m not excited about the book or the conversation the way I used to be. A lot of the time I end up leaving early, or I’ll decide at the last minute not to go at all.\n\nSo yes, I guess the enjoyment is just… muted. The things are still there, I’m still aware I used to like them, but most of the time they feel kind of flat now.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I don’t always call it guilt right away. It’s more like this loop I get into where I keep going back over my marriage, over and over, picking apart conversations or decisions or ways I handled things, and I end up telling myself I must have failed at something really basic.\n\nLike, I know—at least intellectually—I know relationships are complicated, and it’s not usually one person or one moment or one flaw that explains everything. I would say that to a client, honestly. I’d be able to see all the nuance if it were someone else. But when it’s me, it turns into, “Well, what did I miss? What should I have done differently? Was I not patient enough, or attentive enough, or was there something wrong with me that I couldn’t make it work?”\n\nAnd I can recognize that that’s probably not a fair way to think about it, but it still feels… sticky. Like I can’t quite let myself off the hook.\n\nDoctor: How much weight have you lost since this started?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure of the exact number, because I haven’t been tracking it carefully, but I have lost some weight without trying. Enough that my clothes feel a little looser, and I noticed it on the scale when I happened to check.\n\nIt’s tied up with my appetite, I think. For months it’s been really inconsistent. Some evenings I just don’t make dinner at all—partly because I’m not hungry, and partly because cooking for just myself feels kind of pointless, like too much effort for something I don’t even want. Then other nights I’ll realize I haven’t eaten much and I’ll just pick at crackers or pour a bowl of cereal, not because I’m enjoying it, just because it’s there and it doesn’t require cooking.\n\nSo I can’t give you a precise “I lost X pounds,” but yes, there’s been weight loss, and it wasn’t intentional. Honestly, that was one of the things that finally made me think I should come in.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or on edge a lot lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it as anxious exactly. I mean, I do get tense sometimes, but I haven’t really been walking around feeling keyed up or panicky, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s more that I feel worn down than on edge. I don’t notice my heart racing or that kind of constant nervous energy. Sometimes I’ll overthink things, but I don’t know if that’s anxiety or just me getting stuck in my own head.\n\nSo… no, not a lot, I don’t think. Not in the way I hear people describe being anxious all the time.\n\nDoctor: Would you say there are any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say yes in a straightforward way, but… not really, no. I mean, I still do some of the things I used to enjoy, but it’s more like I’m going through the motions than actually looking forward to them.\n\nLike my garden, for example. I used to really love being out there—checking what was coming up, fussing over the plants, watering in the evening. It used to feel peaceful. Now I’ll stand there with the hose and do it because if I don’t, things will die, but I don’t get that same little lift from it. Sometimes I put it off until it’s almost too late, and then I feel bad and go out just to keep it from getting worse.\n\nAnd book club… I still show up sometimes because I know it’s probably good for me to be around people, and I don’t want to just disappear completely. But I don’t really feel excited about the book or the conversation the way I used to. I’ll sit there and listen, maybe say a few things, but part of me is just waiting until it feels acceptable to leave. A couple of times I’ve gone home early, and other times I don’t even get myself out the door.\n\nSo there are things I still technically do, but pleasure is… I don’t know, it feels muted. Flat. I’m not really anticipating things the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about not wanting to be alive or wishing you could just disappear?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, yes, but I want to be clear about what I mean, because even saying it out loud makes me nervous.\n\nI don’t have a plan to hurt myself. I haven’t been thinking through ways to do anything, and I’m not sitting there intending to act on it. But I do sometimes catch myself thinking that it would be a relief if I just didn’t wake up. Or like… not that I want to do something, exactly, but that I wish I could disappear for a while, or not have to keep waking up into the same heaviness.\n\nAnd that scares me. Partly because of my work, I think—I know if a client said that to me, I wouldn’t just brush past it. I’d take it seriously. So then when it’s my own thought, I try to minimize it at first, like, “Oh, I’m just exhausted,” but I know enough to know that it matters.\n\nSo… yes, those thoughts have been there sometimes. No plan, but the “not waking up would be a relief” thought has come up, and I don’t like that it has.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a while. I think the clearest marker for me is when the divorce was finalized. That was about a year and seven months ago now.\n\nI don’t mean that every single day has looked exactly the same since then, but that’s when this version of me really seemed to settle in. Before that, things were hard, obviously, but after it was final it felt like there was this shift—like I expected some kind of relief or fresh start, and instead I just felt… stuck with it. So, roughly a year and seven months.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a period of two weeks or more where you felt depressed and lost interest in activities nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes. Definitely more than two weeks. It’s been going on since the divorce was finalized, which was about a year and seven months ago now.\n\nI mean, there have been small variations day to day, but the overall depressed feeling and the loss of interest have been there for a long time—long enough that it’s hard to think of it as just a bad patch anymore. It’s really been since then that things started feeling consistently flat.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself blaming yourself for your divorce?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. More than I probably want to admit.\n\nI keep going back over the marriage in my head, like I’m reviewing a case file or something, except it’s my own life, so there’s no distance from it. I’ll replay conversations, decisions, moments where maybe I should have said something differently or noticed something earlier or been more patient, more honest, more… whatever. And then it turns into this feeling that I must have failed at something really basic. Like I couldn’t manage to keep a marriage together, or I missed some essential skill everyone else seems to have.\n\nPart of me knows that’s not completely fair. I mean, I know relationships are complicated, and it isn’t usually one person causing everything or one person who could have fixed everything. I would say that to anyone else without hesitation. But when it’s me, I don’t seem to let myself off that easily. I keep landing back on, “What did I do wrong?” or “How did I let it get there?” So yes, I blame myself a lot, even when another part of me knows it’s more complicated than that.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how much weight you've lost since your appetite changed?\n\nPatient: I haven’t kept an exact number, which is part of the problem—I’ve been sort of avoiding looking too closely. But I have lost some weight without trying. My clothes are looser, and the scale has been down when I’ve checked.\n\nIt’s not like I made a plan to eat less or anything. My appetite has just been really inconsistent for months. There are stretches where I’ll realize I’ve skipped dinner, not because I’m busy in a productive way, but because the idea of cooking for myself feels… pointless, almost. And then other nights I’ll just pick at crackers or cereal, something that doesn’t feel like a real meal but is all I can manage.\n\nSo in terms of an estimate, I’d say it’s been enough to notice, but I’m not totally sure of the exact amount.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your divorce is something you could have prevented if you had acted differently?\n\nPatient: I go back and forth on that a lot. Intellectually, I can say, “No, it probably wasn’t that simple.” I mean, marriages are made up of two people, years of patterns, misunderstandings, choices on both sides… I know that. I would probably tell a client or a friend that it’s not fair to reduce something that complicated to one person doing one thing differently.\n\nBut when I’m alone with it, it doesn’t feel that balanced. I keep replaying the marriage in my head, almost scene by scene, and I’ll land on these moments where I think, if I had just handled that better, or been different somehow, maybe it wouldn’t have ended. And then it becomes less about one specific mistake and more this awful feeling that I must have failed at something basic. Like there was some fundamental part of being a partner that I just didn’t get right.\n\nSo… yes, part of me does think I could have prevented it if I’d acted differently. Or at least I keep telling myself that. Even though another part of me knows relationships are more complicated than that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself thinking that you are to blame for the end of your marriage?\n\nPatient: Yes. I know I’ve kind of said this already, but yes… I do come back to that thought a lot.\n\nIt’s not always in a clean, logical way, like, “Here is the exact thing I did wrong.” It’s more this loop I get stuck in, where I replay the marriage over and over and start picking apart little moments—things I said, things I didn’t say, times I withdrew, times I pushed too hard, times I should have understood something sooner. And after a while it all collapses into this feeling that I must have failed at something really basic. Like if I had been a better partner, or handled things differently, maybe it wouldn’t have ended.\n\nAnd I do know, at least somewhere in my head, that relationships are more complicated than that. I know it wasn’t just me standing there controlling the whole outcome. But emotionally, I still keep putting it back on myself. Like I should have been able to prevent it somehow.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your divorce is primarily your fault?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes… or at least that’s where my mind goes, even if I know that’s probably too simple.\n\nI keep replaying the marriage, like I’m trying to find the one moment where I could have fixed everything. And it’s never just one moment, really. It turns into this whole list of things I think I should have done better, or understood sooner, or said differently. Then after a while it stops being about specific arguments or decisions and becomes this heavier feeling that I must have failed at something basic—as a partner, as a person in a relationship.\n\nAt the same time, I do know relationships are more complicated than that. I know it wasn’t only me. But emotionally, it still feels like I was supposed to prevent it somehow, and because I didn’t, I end up blaming myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself wishing you could go back and change how you handled specific situations in your marriage?\n\nPatient: Yes… very often. Probably more than I want to admit.\n\nIt’s like my mind keeps dragging me back to these specific situations, almost as if I’m reviewing a case file or something, trying to identify the exact point where I should have intervened differently. I’ll think, “Why did I say it that way?” or “Why didn’t I listen better then?” or “Why did I shut down instead of staying present?” And sometimes the situations aren’t even huge dramatic moments. They’re small things, conversations in the kitchen, arguments that went nowhere, times I felt hurt and handled it badly, or times I thought I was being reasonable but maybe I was just being defensive.\n\nAnd then it spirals. It starts with one scene, but pretty soon I’m not just wishing I could redo that one conversation. I’m telling myself that I must have failed at something basic in the marriage. Like there was some essential part of being a good partner that I somehow missed. I know, logically, that a marriage is more complicated than a handful of moments I can replay in my head. I know it wasn’t just me, and I know two people create those patterns over time.\n\nBut emotionally… yes. I wish I could go back and change things, because part of me still feels like if I had handled them differently, maybe everything would have turned out differently.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself feeling guilty or blaming yourself for the end of your marriage?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I know I’ve probably circled around this already, but yes, guilt is a big part of it.\n\nIt’s not always that I can point to one specific thing and say, “That was entirely my fault.” It’s more that I keep replaying the marriage in my head, going back over conversations and decisions and patterns, and then I start telling myself that I must have failed at something basic. Like there was some way I was supposed to know better, or be better, or handle things in a way that would have kept it from ending.\n\nAnd part of me does understand that relationships are more complicated than that. I know it wasn’t just one person or one mistake. But emotionally, I keep landing back in this place where I blame myself anyway, like if I had been different somehow, the marriage might not have fallen apart.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a period of at least two weeks where you either had depressed mood most of the day or markedly diminished interest or pleasure in activities nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think so. It’s been more about things feeling flat than me crying all day or anything like that.\n\nI still do the things I used to care about, at least sometimes. Like I’ll go outside and water the garden because the plants need it, and I used to really enjoy that—the quiet, checking what was blooming, feeling like I was tending to something. But lately I’m just sort of standing there with the hose, doing it because it has to be done, not because I’m getting anything from it.\n\nSame with book club. I still show up sometimes, because I don’t want to disappear completely, and part of me thinks maybe if I go I’ll feel like myself again. But I’ll sit there and listen, and everyone’s talking about the book or catching up, and I just feel distant from it. Like I’m present, but not really engaged. A lot of the time I end up leaving early, or I don’t even manage to start getting ready to go.\n\nSo yes, for at least a couple of weeks, and honestly it feels longer than that, most days the things I used to enjoy just don’t really reach me the same way.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost more than 5% of your body weight since your appetite changed?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes… I mean, I haven’t been tracking it as carefully as I probably should, but my clothes are looser, and when I did step on the scale it was down enough that I noticed. It wasn’t intentional.\n\nMy appetite has been all over the place for months now. I wasn’t trying to lose weight, but between not cooking, not wanting much, and sort of grazing instead of eating actual meals, I think I have lost some.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. But lately I’m just sort of standing there with the hose, doing it because it has to be done, not because I’m getting anything from it.\n\nSame with book club. I still show up sometimes, because I don’t want to disappear completely, and part of me thinks maybe if I go I’ll feel like myself again. But I’ll sit there and listen, and everyone’s talking about the book or catching up, and I just feel distant from it. Like I’m present, but not really engaged. A lot of the time I end up leaving early, or I don’t even manage to start getting ready to go.\n\nSo yes, for at least a couple of weeks, and honestly it feels longer than that, most days the things I used to enjoy just don’t really reach me the same way.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost more than 5% of your body weight since your appetite changed?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes… I mean, I haven’t been tracking it as carefully as I probably should, but my clothes are looser, and when I did step on the scale it was down enough that I noticed. It wasn’t intentional.\n\nMy appetite has been all over the place for months now. Some stretches I just skip dinner completely, not in a planned way, more like I get to the end of the day and the idea of making food for just myself feels pointless and exhausting. Then other nights I’ll eat something barely resembling a meal, like crackers or a bowl of cereal, just because it’s there and I don’t have to think about cooking.\n\nThat was actually part of what made me finally come in. It felt like one of those concrete signs that things weren’t just “a rough patch” anymore. I wasn’t trying to lose weight, but between not cooking, not wanting much, and sort of grazing instead of eating actual meals, I think I have lost some."
